# Research: subset-b-000573

Grouped research for ceph-client Devicetree binding YAML schemas covering DMA, DPLL, DSP, DVFS, EDAC, EEPROM, embedded-controller, extcon, and firmware bindings. Each section preserves the source path and is wrapped for deterministic reconciliation into source-tree-aligned per-file reports.

# sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/dma/mediatek,mt7622-hsdma.yaml

**Purpose:** Defines the Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `MediaTek High-Speed DMA Controller`. In this tree it is a DMA engine, DMA router, or DMA-capable IP binding used by dmaengine providers and DMA client phandle specifiers; it constrains how board DTS files describe the hardware and how Linux drivers discover it through OF matching, phandles, and common binding references.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** This is declarative schema rather than executable code. The public ABI surface is `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/dma/mediatek,mt7622-hsdma.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, and the referenced schemas used by dt-schema. Compatible strings are `mediatek,mt7622-hsdma`, `mediatek,mt7623-hsdma`. Required properties are `reg`, `interrupts`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `power-domains`. Important properties include `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `power-domains`, `#dma-cells`. Referenced schemas are `dma-controller.yaml#`. Property roles include `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO, I2C/SPI address, or child register address; `interrupts` for interrupt line routing; `clocks` for clock provider phandles; `clock-names` for clock input names; `power-domains` for power-domain attachment; `#dma-cells` for DMA client specifier width. The top-level closure rule is `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Maintainers: Sean Wang <sean.wang@mediatek.com>.

**Control flow:** Runtime control flow is supplied by Devicetree tooling and kernel subsystems, not by this YAML file. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves references such as `dma-controller.yaml#`, applies `properties`, `required`, enum/const/items limits, and any `allOf` or conditional branches, then validates the inline examples. During boot, a matching DT node is compiled into the DTB, the kernel matches `compatible`, subsystem code reads cells and phandles, and the bound driver consumes resources such as clock provider phandles, genpd power domains. Conditional/schema-composition points include `allOf`, so different compatibles or child-node shapes can activate different required-property sets. It does not define nested child-node schemas.

**State and persistence behavior:** The schema owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS and DTB nodes that satisfy this contract encode hardware identity, resource topology, phandle specifier widths, and child-node layout for long-lived board files. Changing required fields, compatible ordering, cell counts, or closure policy can break existing DTBs even though no kernel state is stored here.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with dmaengine and Devicetree DMA client code. Direct schema dependencies are `dma-controller.yaml#`; resource dependencies visible in the property set include clock provider phandles, genpd power domains. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while client/provider integration is through cell properties, phandle arrays, child schemas, and bus-standard properties declared or inherited here.

**Risks:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, wrong `#*-cells` counts, missing required clocks/resets/interrupts/power domains, stale examples, and conditional branches that accept one SoC variant while rejecting another valid one. Unknown-property policy also matters: `unevaluatedProperties: false` can either catch misspelled DTS properties or allow legacy extension points. For bindings with child nodes or graph/connector references, parent-child address-cell and port layout mistakes can pass driver review only if dtbs_check coverage is weak.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/dma/mediatek,mt7622-hsdma.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` against DTS users of every compatible family. Verify inline examples compile, required-property failures are reported, compatible-specific branches are exercised, inherited schemas resolve, and drivers still probe with the resources described here. The file includes 1 example block(s), which are part of the dt-schema validation signal.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/dma/mediatek,mt7622-hsdma.yaml -->

Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS and DTB nodes that satisfy this contract encode hardware identity, resource topology, phandle specifier widths, and child-node layout for long-lived board files. Changing required fields, compatible ordering, cell counts, or closure policy can break existing DTBs even though no kernel state is stored here.

Direct schema dependencies are `dma-controller.yaml#`; resource dependencies visible in the property set include clock provider phandles, genpd power domains. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while client/provider integration is through cell properties, phandle arrays, child schemas, and bus-standard properties declared or inherited here.

**Risks:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, wrong `#*-cells` counts, missing required clocks/resets/interrupts/power domains, stale examples, and conditional branches that accept one SoC variant while rejecting another valid one. For bindings with child nodes or graph/connector references, parent-child address-cell and port layout mistakes can pass driver review only if dtbs_check coverage is weak.

In this tree it is a DMA engine, DMA router, or DMA-capable IP binding used by dmaengine providers and DMA client phandle specifiers; it constrains how board DTS files describe the hardware and how Linux drivers discover it through OF matching, phandles, and common binding references. The source description says: This describes the devicetree bindings for an external DMA engine to perform memory-to-memory or peripheral-to-memory data transfer capable of supporting 16 channels, implemented in Socionext UniPhier SoCs.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** This is declarative schema rather than executable code. The public ABI surface is `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/dma/socionext,uniphier-xdmac.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, and the referenced schemas used by dt-schema. Compatible strings are `socionext,uniphier-xdmac`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `#dma-cells`, `dma-channels`. Important properties include `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `#dma-cells`, `dma-channels`. Referenced schemas are `dma-controller.yaml#`. Property roles include `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO, I2C/SPI address, or child register address; `interrupts` for interrupt line routing; `#dma-cells` for DMA client specifier width; `dma-channels` for number of DMA channels. The top-level closure rule is `additionalProperties: false`. Maintainers: Kunihiko Hayashi <hayashi.kunihiko@socionext.com>.

**Control flow:** Runtime control flow is supplied by Devicetree tooling and kernel subsystems, not by this YAML file. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves references such as `dma-controller.yaml#`, applies `properties`, `required`, enum/const/items limits, and any `allOf` or conditional branches, then validates the inline examples. During boot, a matching DT node is compiled into the DTB, the kernel matches `compatible`, subsystem code reads cells and phandles, and the bound driver consumes resources such as platform driver `of_match_table` binding through the compatible string. Conditional/schema-composition points include `allOf`, so different compatibles or child-node shapes can activate different required-property sets. It does not define nested child-node schemas.

**State and persistence behavior:** The schema owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS and DTB nodes that satisfy this contract encode hardware identity, resource topology, phandle specifier widths, and child-node layout for long-lived board files. Changing required fields, compatible ordering, cell counts, or closure policy can break existing DTBs even though no kernel state is stored here.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with dmaengine and Devicetree DMA client code. Direct schema dependencies are `dma-controller.yaml#`; resource dependencies visible in the property set include platform driver `of_match_table` binding through the compatible string. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while client/provider integration is through cell properties, phandle arrays, child schemas, and bus-standard properties declared or inherited here.

**Risks:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, wrong `#*-cells` counts, missing required clocks/resets/interrupts/power domains, stale examples, and conditional branches that accept one SoC variant while rejecting another valid one. Unknown-property policy also matters: `additionalProperties: false` can either catch misspelled DTS properties or allow legacy extension points. For bindings with child nodes or graph/connector references, parent-child address-cell and port layout mistakes can pass driver review only if dtbs_check coverage is weak.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/dma/socionext,uniphier-xdmac.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` against DTS users of every compatible family. Verify inline examples compile, required-property failures are reported, compatible-specific branches are exercised, inherited schemas resolve, and drivers still probe with the resources described here. The file includes 1 example block(s), which are part of the dt-schema validation signal.

**Purpose:** Defines the Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `Xilinx AXI VDMA, DMA, CDMA and MCDMA IP`. In this tree it is a DMA engine, DMA router, or DMA-capable IP binding used by dmaengine providers and DMA client phandle specifiers; it constrains how board DTS files describe the hardware and how Linux drivers discover it through OF matching, phandles, and common binding references. The source description says: Xilinx AXI VDMA engine, it does transfers between memory and video devices. It can be configured to have one channel or two channels. If configured as two channels, one is to transmit to the video device and another is to receive from the video device. Xilinx AXI DMA engine, it does transfers between memory and AXI4 stream target devices. It can be configure...

**Important APIs/types/functions:** This is declarative schema rather than executable code. The public ABI surface is `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/dma/xilinx/xlnx,axi-dma.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, and the referenced schemas used by dt-schema. Compatible strings are `xlnx,axi-cdma-1.00.a`, `xlnx,axi-dma-1.00.a`, `xlnx,axi-mcdma-1.00.a`, `xlnx,axi-vdma-1.00.a`. Required properties are `#dma-cells`, `reg`, `xlnx,addrwidth`, `dma-ranges`, `clocks`, `clock-names`. Important properties include `compatible`, `reg`, `#dma-cells`, `#address-cells`, `#size-cells`, `interrupts`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `dma-ranges`, `xlnx,addrwidth`, `xlnx,num-fstores`, `xlnx,flush-fsync`, `xlnx,sg-length-width`, `xlnx,irq-delay`, `xlnx,axistream-connected`. Referenced schemas are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `../dma-controller.yaml#`. Property roles include `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO, I2C/SPI address, or child register address; `#dma-cells` for DMA client specifier width; `#address-cells` for child-node address cell width; `#size-cells` for child-node size cell width; `interrupts` for interrupt line routing; `clocks` for clock provider phandles; `clock-names` for clock input names. The top-level closure rule is `unevaluatedProperties: false`. Maintainers: Radhey Shyam Pandey <radhey.shyam.pandey@amd.com>, Abin Joseph <abin.joseph@amd.com>.

**Control flow:** Runtime control flow is supplied by Devicetree tooling and kernel subsystems, not by this YAML file. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves references such as `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `../dma-controller.yaml#`, applies `properties`, `required`, enum/const/items limits, and any `allOf` or conditional branches, then validates the inline examples. During boot, a matching DT node is compiled into the DTB, the kernel matches `compatible`, subsystem code reads cells and phandles, and the bound driver consumes resources such as clock provider phandles. Conditional/schema-composition points include `allOf`, `patternProperties`, `conditional branches`, so different compatibles or child-node shapes can activate different required-property sets. Nested or patterned child schema surfaces include `xlnx,addrwidth`, `xlnx,num-fstores`, `xlnx,sg-length-width`, `xlnx,irq-delay`, `^dma-channel(-mm2s|-s2mm)?$`.

**State and persistence behavior:** The schema owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS and DTB nodes that satisfy this contract encode hardware identity, resource topology, phandle specifier widths, and child-node layout for long-lived board files. Changing required fields, compatible ordering, cell counts, or closure policy can break existing DTBs even though no kernel state is stored here.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with dmaengine and Devicetree DMA client code. Direct schema dependencies are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `../dma-controller.yaml#`; resource dependencies visible in the property set include clock provider phandles. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while client/provider integration is through cell properties, phandle arrays, child schemas, and bus-standard properties declared or inherited here.

**Risks:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, wrong `#*-cells` counts, missing required clocks/resets/interrupts/power domains, stale examples, and conditional branches that accept one SoC variant while rejecting another valid one. Unknown-property policy also matters: `unevaluatedProperties: false` can either catch misspelled DTS properties or allow legacy extension points. For bindings with child nodes or graph/connector references, parent-child address-cell and port layout mistakes can pass driver review only if dtbs_check coverage is weak.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/dma/xilinx/xlnx,axi-dma.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` against DTS users of every compatible family. Verify inline examples compile, required-property failures are reported, compatible-specific branches are exercised, inherited schemas resolve, and drivers still probe with the resources described here. The file includes 2 example block(s), which are part of the dt-schema validation signal.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/dma/xilinx/xlnx,axi-dma.yaml -->

**Purpose:** Defines the Devicetree JSON-schema contract for `Lenovo Thinkpad T14s Embedded Controller`. In this tree it is a embedded controller or board-management controller binding used to model management firmware and its child functions; it constrains how board DTS files describe the hardware and how Linux drivers discover it through OF matching, phandles, and common binding references. The source description says: The Qualcomm Snapdragon-based Lenovo Thinkpad T14s has an Embedded Controller (EC) which handles things such as keyboard backlight, LEDs or non-standard keys.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** This is declarative schema rather than executable code. The public ABI surface is `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/embedded-controller/lenovo,thinkpad-t14s-ec.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, and the referenced schemas used by dt-schema. Compatible strings are `lenovo,thinkpad-t14s-ec`. Required properties are `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`. Important properties include `compatible`, `reg`, `interrupts`, `wakeup-source`. Referenced schemas are none declared. Property roles include `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO, I2C/SPI address, or child register address; `interrupts` for interrupt line routing; `wakeup-source` for system wake capability. The top-level closure rule is `additionalProperties: false`. Maintainers: Sebastian Reichel <sre@kernel.org>.

**Control flow:** Runtime control flow is supplied by Devicetree tooling and kernel subsystems, not by this YAML file. `dt_binding_check` loads the core meta-schema, resolves references such as none declared, applies `properties`, `required`, enum/const/items limits, and any `allOf` or conditional branches, then validates the inline examples. During boot, a matching DT node is compiled into the DTB, the kernel matches `compatible`, subsystem code reads cells and phandles, and the bound driver consumes resources such as platform driver `of_match_table` binding through the compatible string. It has no nested conditional schema branches beyond direct property validation. It does not define nested child-node schemas.

**State and persistence behavior:** The schema owns no mutable runtime state and writes no persistent data. Its persistent effect is ABI stability: DTS and DTB nodes that satisfy this contract encode hardware identity, resource topology, phandle specifier widths, and child-node layout for long-lived board files. Changing required fields, compatible ordering, cell counts, or closure policy can break existing DTBs even though no kernel state is stored here.

**Dependencies and integration points:** Integrated with MFD, EC, GPIO, interrupt, connector, PWM, watchdog, hwmon, and board-management drivers. Direct schema dependencies are none declared; resource dependencies visible in the property set include platform driver `of_match_table` binding through the compatible string. Driver integration is through `compatible`, while client/provider integration is through cell properties, phandle arrays, child schemas, and bus-standard properties declared or inherited here.

**Risks:** Main risks are schema ABI drift, incomplete compatible coverage, wrong `#*-cells` counts, missing required clocks/resets/interrupts/power domains, stale examples, and conditional branches that accept one SoC variant while rejecting another valid one. Unknown-property policy also matters: `additionalProperties: false` can either catch misspelled DTS properties or allow legacy extension points. For bindings with child nodes or graph/connector references, parent-child address-cell and port layout mistakes can pass driver review only if dtbs_check coverage is weak.

**Test signals:** Run `make dt_binding_check DT_SCHEMA_FILES=sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/embedded-controller/lenovo,thinkpad-t14s-ec.yaml` and `make dtbs_check` against DTS users of every compatible family. Verify inline examples compile, required-property failures are reported, compatible-specific branches are exercised, inherited schemas resolve, and drivers still probe with the resources described here. The file includes 1 example block(s), which are part of the dt-schema validation signal.
<!-- END_FILE_RESEARCH: sources/distributed-fs/ceph-client/Documentation/devicetree/bindings/embedded-controller/lenovo,thinkpad-t14s-ec.yaml -->

In this tree it is a Devicetree binding schema; it constrains how board DTS files describe the hardware and how Linux drivers discover it through OF matching, phandles, and common binding references. The source description says: A more detailed multi-line description of the binding. Details about the hardware device and any links to datasheets can go here. Literal blocks are marked with the '|' at the beginning. The end is marked by indentation less than the first line of the literal block. Lines also cannot begin with a tab character.

**Important APIs/types/functions:** This is declarative schema rather than executable code. The public ABI surface is `$id` `http://devicetree.org/schemas/example-schema.yaml#`, `$schema` `http://devicetree.org/meta-schemas/core.yaml#`, `title`, `maintainers`, `properties`, `required`, and the referenced schemas used by dt-schema. Important properties include `compatible`, `reg`, `reg-names`, `clocks`, `clock-names`, `interrupts`, `interrupt-names`, `#interrupt-cells`, `interrupt-controller`, `clock-frequency`, `foo-gpios`, `foo-supply`, `vendor,int-property`, `vendor,bool-property`, `vendor,string-array-property`, `vendor,property-in-standard-units-microvolt`, and 3 more. Referenced schemas are `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/string-array`, `/schemas/types.yaml#/definitions/uint32-array`. Property roles include `compatible` for driver match key and hardware ABI identifier; `reg` for MMIO, I2C/SPI address, or child register address; `clocks` for clock provider phandles; `clock-names` for clock input names; `interrupts` for interrupt line routing; `interrupt-names` for semantic interrupt labels; `#interrupt-cells` for interrupt specifier width; `interrupt-controller` for interrupt provider role. Nested or patterned child schema surfaces include `vendor,int-property`, `vendor,string-array-property`, `vendor,int-array-variable-length-and-constrained-values`, `child-node`, `child-node-with-own-schema`.

Unknown-property policy also matters: `additionalProperties: false` can either catch misspelled DTS properties or allow legacy extension points. For bindings with child nodes or graph/connector references, parent-child address-cell and port layout mistakes can pass driver review only if dtbs_check coverage is weak.
